Locale&quot;]}],&quot;url&quot;:[0,&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/voidzero-joins-cloudflare&quot;],&quot;metadata&quot;:[0,{&quot;title&quot;:[0,&quot;VoidZero is joining Cloudflare&quot;],&quot;description&quot;:[0,&quot;VoidZero, the team behind Vite, Vitest, Rolldown, Oxc, and Vite+, is joining Cloudflare. Vite stays open source, vendor-agnostic, and built for everyone.\n&quot;],&quot;imgPreview&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/77PFBBDzFzIVtsPi4GXm5g/ea71873167012a47518dc593033032c6/BLOG-VOID_OG.png&quot;]}],&quot;publicly_index&quot;:[0,true]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;5kM0Nfgzw6beOD5mSzIqw4&quot;],&quot;title&quot;:[0,&quot;How we built Cloudflare&#39;s data platform and an AI agent on top of it&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;our-unified-data-platform&quot;],&quot;excerpt&quot;:[0,&quot;Hereâs how we built Town Lake, Cloudflare&#39;s unified analytics platform, alongside Skipper, an internal AI agent running on top of it.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;Cloudflare processes more than a billion events every second. Our network spans 330+ cities in 120+ countries. Behind every HTTP request, every Worker invocation, every R2 read operation, there is data, and a lot of it.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For years, that data was not very easy to access. It lived in dozens of production databases, ClickHouse clusters, Kafka streams, Google Cloud buckets, BigQuery datasets, and a long tail of pipelines. To answer a simple question like &amp;quot;How many domains that signed up today are in the Top 100 by traffic?&amp;quot;, an analyst at Cloudflare had to know which system to ask, what credentials to use, what query language to write, and whether the data they were looking at was sampled, fresh, or seven-days stale. As a result, it was difficult to glean informed insights from the data.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To solve this problem, we built two in-house tools: Town Lake, Cloudflare&amp;#39;s unified data analytics platform, and Skipper, an AI data agent that runs on top of it. Town Lake is a single SQL interface to everything Cloudflare knows, and Skipper is how anyone at Cloudflare can ask questions in plain English and get correct, auditable answers back in seconds.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This is the story of how we built both.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;the-shape-of-the-problem\&quot;&gt;The shape of the problem&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-shape-of-the-problem\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;If you have ever worked at a company that went through a hyper-growth period, you know what data sprawl looks like. Ours had a few specific symptoms:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Too many disparate systems.&lt;/b&gt; A product engineer who wanted to investigate a customer issue might need to query Postgres for account metadata, ClickHouse for analytics events, BigQuery for usage rollups, R2 for raw logs, and Kafka topics for real-time signals. Each system had its own credentials, its own language, and its own retention policy.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Sampled data.&lt;/b&gt; This is fine for dashboards, but doesnât work for domains like billing. Our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-we-make-sense-of-too-much-data\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;analytics pipeline&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; downsamples to handle 700M+ events per second. That is the right behavior when you want an analytics dashboard to load, but itâs exactly the wrong behavior when you are trying to compute someoneâs usage required to issue an invoice.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;External dependencies for internal data.&lt;/b&gt; Parts of our previous internal reporting stack were powered by external vendors. Beyond the cost, we had a hard external dependency on another cloud for some of our critical data.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;No one could find the data.&lt;/b&gt; Even if you had all the right credentials, you needed to know that the right table for &amp;quot;Billable Workers requests by account&amp;quot; lived in a specific ClickHouse cluster, in a specific schema, joined to a specific Postgres dimension table, and that the join required an obscure customer ID translation. There was too much tribal knowledge.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;We had a cultural challenge too: data infrastructure had historically been treated as a back-office function that was in service of the business, rather than critical infrastructure in its own right.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;what-we-wanted\&quot;&gt;What we wanted&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#what-we-wanted\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We wanted to create one place where anyone at the company with appropriate permissions and a need to know could get answers to questions about Cloudflare: âShow me the top 100 customers by revenue in the last quarterâ, âList all Bot Management ML scoring events with score &amp;gt; 0.9 in the last 48 hours coming from a specific ASNâ, âFind the Top 100 billing support tickets from customers who have spent &amp;gt;$100â, etc.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We wanted that place to give fresh, accurate, unsampled data for the queries that need it (like billing or security investigations) and fast, downsampled data for the queries that don&amp;#39;t (like dashboards or exploration).&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We wanted security and governance baked in, with personally identifiable information (PII) detected automatically, and sensitive tables locked down by default. All access should be auditable, and have time-bounded permission grants so that users could only access data when they were actively working on tasks that required it.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We wanted it to be built on Cloudflareâs own platform: &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/products/r2/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;R2&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for storage, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/products/workers/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Workers&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for compute, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/products/access/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare Access&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for authentication, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/products/workflows/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Workflows&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for orchestration. If we were going to make a major investment in our data infrastructure, it was going to be built on the same products we sell to customers.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;And we wanted, eventually, an interface that did not require knowing any SQL. The goal was to empower anyone at the company with appropriate permissions and a need to know to look at the stream of data flowing through our network, not just analysts.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;That last requirement is what became Skipper.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;town-lake-the-platform\&quot;&gt;Town Lake, the platform&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#town-lake-the-platform\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;At its core, our data platformâs architecture is a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_lakehouse\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;data lakehouse&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;: a query engine that reads from object storage, with a metadata layer that makes the storage behave like a database. We call it Town Lake, after its namesake in Austin, Texas.&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card \&quot;&gt;\n            \n            &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/47E8uC26eDC4XrOibNlGCi/4b1e083bb76de4f09404bdac07e5f03a/image5.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1273\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n            \n            &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;Its most important components are:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Query engine.&lt;/b&gt; We chose Apache Trino for that: a single SQL query can join a Postgres table, a ClickHouse table, and an Iceberg table on R2 without a need to materialize the intermediate results into a different system. A query that asks &amp;quot;what are the top 100 paying customers by Workers requests this week&amp;quot; compiles into a plan that pushes filters into ClickHouse, joins against an account dimension in Postgres, and ranks against billing rollups in R2, all in one go.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;R2 Data Catalog,&lt;/b&gt; our managed Apache Iceberg service, is where the cold and warm data lives. Iceberg gives us schema evolution, time travel, partition evolution, and the ability to compact data as it ages. Per-minute usage from last week becomes hourly, hourly from last quarter becomes daily, etc. The storage cost decreases as recency does, while the data stays queryable. Parquet files in R2 are much cheaper compared to keeping the same data in an OLAP database.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;DataHub&lt;/b&gt; is our metadata catalog. Every table, column, owner, lineage edge, and glossary term lives there. When a user asks &amp;quot;what&amp;#39;s in &lt;code&gt;townlake.dim.accounts&lt;/code&gt;,&amp;quot; DataHub provides an answer, including the table description, the column descriptions, the owning team, the upstream tables that feed it, and the downstream tables that consume it.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Lifeguard&lt;/b&gt; is our access control service: it stores access rules in D1, dynamically pulls user and group membership from our internal access management system, and renders a combined JSON policy that Trino reads over HTTP. Lifeguard also feeds basic access information to Skipper and the Gateway, so users get blocked at the front door rather than at query time.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Skimmer&lt;/b&gt; is a PII detection scanner. It runs continuously, samples rows from every column in every table, and uses Workers AI to classify whether each column contains PII. It does this in two passes: first, a fast per-column classifier; then, if anything is flagged, an agentic second pass that gets full table context and can query Trino directly to verify. Findings flow into DataHub and into Lifeguard&amp;#39;s allowlist to allow human-in-the-loop review.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Transformer&lt;/b&gt; is our ELT (extract, load, transform) engine built on Workflows. Users define a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) of SQL transformations with YAML frontmatter (target table, materialization mode, dependencies, schedule). Transformer compiles the graph and runs it on Trino, with state managed by Durable Objects, definitions stored in R2, and run history in D1.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Ingestion&lt;/b&gt; is the bridge from operational systems into the lake. An orchestrator runs as a long-lived Kubernetes deployment, reads pipeline configs, and spawns short-lived worker jobs to extract from Postgres or ClickHouse, transform to Parquet, and load into R2 as Iceberg tables. Each pipeline runs as either full-replace or incremental-append.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/3eVzLYLm870nH9S3qVmGD9/6c8d81f3c07a2edcec1d83648d1edb1f/image3.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;573\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;default-closed-governance-by-construction\&quot;&gt;Default-closed: governance by construction&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#default-closed-governance-by-construction\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;A real concern when you build a unified data platform is that you have just built a large sensitive-data surface. The traditional answer to this is: open by default, restrict by exception. Allow access to everything, then audit and lock down sensitive tables when someone notices.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Town Lake takes the opposite approach. Tables are inaccessible for querying until they have been reviewed. When a new database is connected to Trino or a new table is created, Skimmer scans it, classifies its columns, and registers it in the central allowlist as pending. Until a reviewer approves the table, and the specific columns within it, users can&amp;#39;t query it. This sounds painful, and it would be, except for two things.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;First, it&amp;#39;s automated. Skimmer&amp;#39;s classifier is reasonably good: it catches obvious PII (emails, IPs, names, phone numbers) and the long tail of non-obvious sensitive data (API tokens that match certain prefixes, opaque IDs that can be traced back to users). Reviewers see what was detected and either approve, override, or deny. Most reviews take seconds.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Second, the workflow is self-serve. If you query a table you don&amp;#39;t have access to, the error message is not &amp;quot;permission denied.&amp;quot; It&amp;#39;s &amp;quot;this table needs review, click here to request one.&amp;quot; Skipper, the AI agent, will even suggest the right &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/access-management/role-based-access-control-rbac/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;RBAC&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; group to request and link you straight to it.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We separate schema discovery from data access. Users can see what tables exist, but unreviewed columns are hidden from &lt;code&gt;DESCRIBE&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;SHOW COLUMNS&lt;/code&gt; and from &lt;code&gt;SELECT *&lt;/code&gt;. That subtle distinction matters: it means a new unreviewed column doesn&amp;#39;t break existing dashboards built on the rest of an approved table.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;PII is opt-in per session. By default, Trino redacts sensitive columns before they ever hit your screen. If you have a legitimate need for raw PII (e.g., fraud investigation), you flip the bit on the session, your permissions are checked, and the redaction is lifted. The flip and every query is logged.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;skipper-the-ai-data-agent\&quot;&gt;Skipper: the AI data agent&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#skipper-the-ai-data-agent\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;A query engine alone isnât enough these days. SQL is still a barrier, as is knowing which of tens of thousands of tables to query â you need to know the canonical schema.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Skipper is our take on a conversational AI agent that goes from natural-language question to validated answer, grounded in the company&amp;#39;s actual data, code, and institutional knowledge. We built it on top of Town Lake and on top of our developer platform: Workers, Workers AI, Durable Objects, D1, R2, Workflows, KV.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The interface is a chat box. Ask a question:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;i&gt;Show me the top 10 customers by R2 storage cost in the last 30 days, and the change versus the previous 30 days.&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;Skipper finds the right tables (DataHub search), pulls their schemas and lineage, writes the SQL, submits it to Trino, polls for results, and shows you a table or a chart. Follow up:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;i&gt;Now break it down by region, and ignore internal Cloudflare accounts.&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;It carries the context, refines the query, and reruns it. If something looks wrong, e.g., a join produced zero rows or a filter excluded what you expected, then Skipper investigates, adjusts, and tries again, in the closed-loop reasoning. The hard part was having the right context.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Skipper can also package charts into dashboards that can be shared internally and embedded into other internal applications. It also has tools for building transformation graphs via Transformer and for checking access and permissions via Lifeguard.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Skipper meets its users wherever they are. All of these tools are available via a Worker backed by a built-in agentic harness powered by Workers AI. On the flip side, many of our internal users work via local agentic flows, and Skipperâs tools are additionally available via an MCP server.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;layers-of-context\&quot;&gt;Layers of context&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#layers-of-context\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;An LLM, given a SQL prompt and a list of table names, can hallucinate joins, misuse columns, and confidently produce a number that is completely wrong. We learned this the hard way during early experiments. The fix is multiple layers of grounded context that the model can pull from at retrieval time.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6IEpUwNgEVrmNBMeKjFqRY/84a83ec11b38fa6c7595db2dc4fcb334/image2.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1824\&quot; height=\&quot;1204\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;Layer 1: Schema and usage metadata. DataHub knows every column, every type, every primary key, every foreign key for every table. It also knows which tables are commonly joined together based on historical query patterns. Skipper&amp;#39;s &lt;code&gt;search_datasets&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;get_entity_details&lt;/code&gt; tools surface this directly.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Layer 2: Human annotations. When the team that owns &lt;code&gt;dim.accounts&lt;/code&gt; writes a description like &lt;i&gt;&amp;quot;Account-level entity. One row per account_id. Every account belongs to exactly one customer (via customer_id FK),&amp;quot;&lt;/i&gt; that description lives in DataHub and ends up in Skipper&amp;#39;s context. Tags like &lt;code&gt;curated&lt;/code&gt; mark validated tables that Skipper should prefer over scratch space.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Layer 3: Code-derived knowledge. Some of the most valuable context is not in any catalog: it&amp;#39;s in the SQL that produces the table. The Transformer pipeline emits per-node &lt;code&gt;.meta.json&lt;/code&gt; documentation to DataHub on every successful run. So when Skipper looks at &lt;code&gt;fct.billings_allocated&lt;/code&gt;, it doesn&amp;#39;t just see the schema; it sees that this is a pre-joined fact table built from &lt;code&gt;dim.accounts&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;dim.customers&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;seed.product_classification&lt;/code&gt;, with its &lt;code&gt;alloc_amount&lt;/code&gt; column computed as &lt;code&gt;billed_amount / 12 for annual; billed_amount for monthly&lt;/code&gt;. That&amp;#39;s the kind of nuance that separates a correct answer from a confidently wrong one.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Layer 4: Curated data models. We maintain a small set of &amp;quot;data model&amp;quot; pages: short, human-written documents that describe how to think about billing, customers, accounts, and zones. &lt;i&gt;&amp;quot;Prefer tables tagged &amp;#39;curated&amp;#39;. Avoid &lt;/i&gt;&lt;code&gt;&lt;i&gt;scratch_r2&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;i&gt; and tables tagged &amp;#39;internal&amp;#39;. Search with data model terms (e.g., &amp;#39;billing product revenue&amp;#39;) not natural language.&amp;quot;&lt;/i&gt; These are surfaced as MCP resources that the agent can pull when the question matches.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Layer 5: Runtime introspection. When everything else fails, Skipper can issue live queries to Trino: &lt;code&gt;DESCRIBE table, SELECT DISTINCT col LIMIT 20, SELECT COUNT(*)&lt;/code&gt;. It uses these sparingly as runtime context is expensive, but it&amp;#39;s the safety net that makes the rest of the system robust.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;skipper-as-mcp-code-mode\&quot;&gt;Skipper as MCP: Code Mode&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#skipper-as-mcp-code-mode\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;One specific implementation detail is worth pulling out, because it is uniquely a Cloudflare-shaped solution.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;When you build an AI agent with tools, the standard pattern is to define the tools in your prompt, let the model call them one at a time, parse the response, execute, and return results. This is fine, but it is chatty: a five-tool workflow is five model round-trips, each of which has to re-establish context.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For our MCP server, we use &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/code-mode/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Code Mode&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Instead of defining 30 individual tools, we expose two: &lt;code&gt;search&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;execute&lt;/code&gt;. The model writes a JavaScript snippet that calls our entire toolset programmatically:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-JavaScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-JavaScript\&quot;&gt;const datasets = await skipper.search_datasets({ query: &amp;quot;billing product revenue&amp;quot; })\nconst queryId = await skipper.start_query({ sql: &amp;quot;SELECT ...&amp;quot; })\nconst results = await skipper.fetch_results({ queryId, mode: &amp;quot;inject&amp;quot; })\nreturn skipper.create_chart({ chartType: &amp;quot;bar&amp;quot;, data: results.rows, ... })&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;That JavaScript runs in a sandboxed Dynamic Worker isolate via &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/runtime-apis/bindings/worker-loader/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;WorkerLoader&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. The model gets to express complex multi-step workflows in a single round-trip, in a language it already knows extremely well. It&amp;#39;s faster, it&amp;#39;s cheaper, and the workflows it produces are auditable as code.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;the-security-model-is-the-data-model\&quot;&gt;The security model is the data model&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-security-model-is-the-data-model\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Everything Skipper does runs as the calling user. If you don&amp;#39;t have access to a table, Skipper can&amp;#39;t query it for you. If you ask for PII, your permissions are checked. If a query you save is shared with a teammate, their access is checked at view time, not at save time, because group membership changes.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Shared dashboards have their own twist. They can be embedded in any internal Cloudflare tool with a single placeholder div and a script tag:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-HTML\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-HTML\&quot;&gt;&amp;lt;div data-skipper-dashboard=&amp;quot;dash-123&amp;quot;&amp;gt;&amp;lt;/div&amp;gt;\n&amp;lt;script src=&amp;quot;https://skipper.cloudflare.com/embed.js&amp;quot; async&amp;gt;&amp;lt;/script&amp;gt;&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;The iframe auto-resizes to fit content. Content Security Policy (CSP) &lt;code&gt;frame-ancestors&lt;/code&gt; blocks embedding from anywhere outside the corporate domain. Cloudflare Access still gates the iframe contents, so an unauthenticated viewer hits the Access login page in the iframe rather than seeing the data. Non-owner viewers are checked against the underlying tables: if they don&amp;#39;t have access, they get pointed at the right group to request.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/54J6Qyl8K77YAhOsuCRGEp/55e8090976b45ff77fdfa049731fe303/image4.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1159\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;what-it-powers-really-fast-answers\&quot;&gt;What it powers: really fast answers&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#what-it-powers-really-fast-answers\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Billing.&lt;/b&gt; This was the original use case. Our Billable Usage Dashboard, the customer-facing dashboard that shows pay-as-you-go users exactly what they owe, is powered by a metering pipeline whose source of truth is a set of Iceberg tables in R2, queried via Trino. The dashboard&amp;#39;s API pulls the same compact &lt;code&gt;(date, account_id, metric_name, usage)&lt;/code&gt; rows that the invoicing system uses, so the number on the dashboard matches the number on the bill.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Billing-related queries account for 53% of all queries Town Lake serves: 91,760 queries from 324 distinct Cloudflare employees in a recent measurement period. The 200â300 line legacy SQL queries that used to compute revenue rollups by customer are now five lines.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Business intelligence.&lt;/b&gt; The &amp;quot;top 100 customers by revenue&amp;quot; question takes about three seconds in Skipper now. So does &amp;quot;how many domains that signed up today are in the top 100.&amp;quot; So do most of the data-related questions we used to file Jira tickets for.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Security analytics.&lt;/b&gt; Our Bot Management team uses Town Lake to query ML scoring events with score &amp;gt; 0.9 in the last 48 hours filtered by ASN and geography. Threat researchers have built their own query toolkit on top of it. Trust &amp;amp; Safety pulls signals to help police abuse.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Customer support.&lt;/b&gt; &amp;quot;Find the top 100 billing support tickets from customers who have spent &amp;gt;$100&amp;quot; used to be a multi-day project. Now it&amp;#39;s a Skipper query.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;what-we-have-learned\&quot;&gt;What we have learned&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#what-we-have-learned\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;A few things have surprised us.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Less prompting is more. Early versions of Skipper had elaborate, prescriptive system prompts: &lt;i&gt;&amp;quot;First, use search_datasets. Then, use get_entity_details. Then, use list_schema_fields if needed...&amp;quot;&lt;/i&gt; Quality went down. The model is good at reasoning about analytical workflows; it doesn&amp;#39;t need to be micromanaged. We replaced the prescriptive prompts with high-level guidance and let the model pick its own path. Results got better.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Tool overlap is poison. We initially exposed every variant of every tool: three different &amp;quot;fetch results&amp;quot; tools, two &amp;quot;search&amp;quot; tools, several &amp;quot;list&amp;quot; tools. The model got confused and called the wrong one. We consolidated. Now &lt;code&gt;fetch_results&lt;/code&gt; has a &lt;code&gt;mode&lt;/code&gt; parameter &lt;code&gt;(inject / display / both)&lt;/code&gt; instead of three separate tools. Every tool has a single reason to exist.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Code, not metadata, captures meaning. The biggest accuracy wins came when we started ingesting the actual SQL that produces a table, not just its schema. A &lt;code&gt;customer_type&lt;/code&gt; column with values &lt;code&gt;contract&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;paygo&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;free&lt;/code&gt; looks identical in either context, but the SQL tells you that &lt;code&gt;customer_type&lt;/code&gt; defaults to &lt;code&gt;paygo&lt;/code&gt; when Salesforce data is missing. That kind of context never lives in column descriptions.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Memory matters more than we expected. There is a long tail of corrections that look like &amp;quot;you have to filter for X like this&amp;quot; or &amp;quot;ignore tables tagged Y.&amp;quot; Without a memory layer, the agent rediscovers and re-learns these every conversation. With one, it gets monotonically better at the recurring questions a team actually asks.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The boring infrastructure is the hard part. Trino + Iceberg is not new technology. The hard work is in the boring stuff: per-row access control, default-closed table allowlisting, query auditing, time-bound credentials, PII detection, idempotent ingestion, schema evolution. Those are the things that make a data platform safe to actually use.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;whats-next\&quot;&gt;What&amp;#39;s next&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#whats-next\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We&amp;#39;re expanding the agent surface. Skipper already integrates as an MCP server into any IDE that supports it. The next step is deeper integration with our own internal chat and ticketing systems, so that &amp;quot;ask the data&amp;quot; becomes the natural first move for anyone debugging an incident, scoping a project, or sanity-checking a hypothesis.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We&amp;#39;re investing heavily in the Transformer pipeline. The goal is for any team at Cloudflare to be able to build a curated dataset with a few SQL files and a &lt;code&gt;.meta.json&lt;/code&gt; description, deploy it as a Workflow, get it scheduled and monitored automatically, and have it surface in DataHub and Skipper without any additional work. The idea is self-serve data engineering, with the same shape as self-serve software engineering.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/r2-sql/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;R2 SQL&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, Cloudflare&amp;#39;s serverless, distributed, analytics query engine, is getting more and more robust by the day. As its feature set expands, we plan to move many parts of Town Lakeâs workflow over to it.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The bet we made â that the next breakthrough product comes from someone looking at the data and seeing something nobody else sees â is one we&amp;#39;re still betting on. Town Lake is how we make sure they can find it.&lt;/p&gt;&quot;],&quot;published_at&quot;:[0,&quot;2026-05-28T14:00+01:00&quot;],&quot;updated_at&quot;:[0,&quot;2026-06-02T06:57:24.639Z&quot;],&quot;feature_image&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6zpR8XOPuk3X1BKFC3yjCn/66fd4587109471a850600196898ace78/image1.png&quot;],&quot;tags&quot;:[1,[[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;4ggKJG4JUzqG9FpAPIxwij&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Engineering&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;engineering&quot;]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;2OotqBxtRdi5MuC90AlyxE&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Analytics&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;analytics&quot;]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;7sgZuquWS8hoXTL4pQo4Q3&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Developers ph3 ph0-l fs-20px"><h1 class="f6 f7-l fw4 gray1 pt1 pt3-l mb1">Defend against frontier cyber models: Cloudflare&#x27;s architecture as customer zero</h1><p class="f3 fw5 gray5 db di-l mt2">2026-06-09</p><ul class="author-lists flex pl0 mt4"><li class="list flex items-center pr2 mb1-ns"><a href="/author/rohit-chenna-reddy/" class="static-avatar pr1"><img class="author-profile-image br-100 mr2" alt="Rohit Chenna Reddy" width="62" height="62"/></a><div class="author-name-tooltip"><a href="/author/rohit-chenna-reddy/" class="fw4 f3 no-underline black mr3">Rohit Chenna Reddy</a></div></li><li class="list flex items-center pr2 mb1-ns"><a href="/author/chase-catelli/" class="static-avatar pr1"><img class="author-profile-image br-100 mr2" src="https://blog.cloudflare.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto,dpr=3,width=64,height=64,gravity=face,fit=crop,zoom=0.5/https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/7vYSOtE6dXjx30wPXvWG81/d6fb5b92222c748b0e70d739cbff9148/Chase_Catelli.png" alt="Chase Catelli" width="62" height="62"/></a><div class="author-name-tooltip"><a href="/author/chase-catelli/" class="fw4 f3 no-underline black mr3">Chase Catelli</a></div></li><li class="list flex src="https://blog.cloudflare.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto,dpr=3,width=64,height=64,gravity=face,fit=crop,zoom=0.5/https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/5k6iQ5uNhroseD4aOteFFB/8a815a92940642ec2f0e16f4ffd2463c/Dan_Jones.webp" alt="Dan Jones" width="62" height="62"/></a><div class="author-name-tooltip"><a href="/author/dan-jones/" class="fw4 f3 no-underline black mr3">Dan Jones</a></div></li></ul><section class="post-full-content"><div class="mb2 gray5">8 min read</div><img class="mr2" src="https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6LJuOdPGmy1DD8MOtLlMTl/f13e4d392fad08baebf51b8929eb2c43/image1.png" alt=""/><div class="post-content post-body-content lh-copy gray1"><p></p><p></p><p>A few weeks ago, we <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/cyber-frontier-models/"><u>wrote about Project Glasswing</u></a> and what we observed when we pointed cyber frontier models at our own code. Since then, weâve seen that the part of the post that has resonated most deeply is the argument that the architecture around the vulnerability matters more than the speed of the patch.</p><p>In the conversations we&#39;ve had with CISOs and security teams since, the questions have been consistent: what does our architecture actually look like, what should we monitor for, where do we start, and how can Cloudflare help?</p><p>Before getting into the details: the architecture below is built almost entirely from Cloudflare&#39;s own products, because Cloudflare security is <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/tag/customer-zero/"><u>customer zero</u></a> for the security products we build. The Cloudflare stack already exists in front of our code, employees, and customer-facing applications. If you&#39;re a Cloudflare customer, every layer below is available to you today. If you&#39;re not, the principles still apply to whatever stack you&#39;ve built.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="what-a-cyber-frontier-model-actually-changes">What a cyber frontier model actually changes</h2>
      <a href="#what-a-cyber-frontier-model-actually-changes" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>In the <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/cyber-frontier-models/"><u>previous post</u></a>, we showed how a cyber frontier model like Mythos changes the attackerâs timeline. It can find vulnerabilities, reason through exploit chains, and generate working proofs faster than earlier models. While models like Mythos do not change the shape of an intrusion â reconnaissance, initial access, lateral movement, persistence, and exfiltration still have to happen â the difference is in the speed and scale. When pointed at the open web, a model can find and hit low-hanging fruit quickly. Against a hardened target, it still has to probe, and adapt, and it often produces more noise than a careful human operator would.</p><p>Discovery, exploit chain construction, and proof-of-concept generation used to be the gating constraints on producing a working attack. A frontier model handles all three in a fraction of the time. Work that used to be slow and methodical is now fast and indiscriminate.</p><p>While AI is accelerating how fast developer teams at Cloudflare and many other companies can ship code, the security teamâs work has not compressed the same way. An attacker only needs one opening to get in, while security teams need to find and close them all. Writing a fix, regressing it, and shipping it without breaking the code around it has constraints that AI doesn&#39;t remove. We learned this the hard way when we let an AI coding assistant write its own patches against our own bugs, as we described at the end of the previous <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/cyber-frontier-models/"><u>post</u></a>. Some of those patches fixed the original bug while quietly breaking something else the code depended on.</p><p>As these models become more competent and capable, our main focus from a threat standpoint comes down to three things. Each one shapes the architecture we walk through in the rest of this post.</p><ul><li><p>The first is the <b>speed of discovery.</b>Â Frontier models make it easier to search large bodies of public code, including the open-source libraries that many companies depend on. That does not mean every bug in a library is exploitable, or that library bugs are where most vulnerabilities live. Exploitability still depends on how the code is used, whether attacker-controlled input can reach the vulnerable path, and the protections that sit around it. But widely used open-source libraries and frameworks give attackers a shared surface to study at scale. When a real, reachable vulnerability exists there, a model can help find it, reason about possible exploit paths, and generate proof-of-concept variants faster than maintainers and defenders can review every downstream use. The gap between when an attacker discovers a vulnerability and when defenders learn it exists is what worries us most. If you are not running these models against your own code, it is safe to assume someone else is.</p></li><li><p>The second is <b>exploit</b> <b>volume and adaptation.</b> A model can produce thousands of variations of a single exploit and run reconnaissance at the same scale. All that volume gives an attacker an advantage, but it wonât necessarily get them past signature-based detections. Many of those iterations will have the same underlying signature, so a rule that catches the first one will catch the rest. Adaptation is how they will get past signature-based detections. Ask a model to show you a SQL injection, and it will return a textbook example. Tell it there is a WAF in the way, and it will start probing, learning what gets blocked, and rewriting the payload until it can slip past the rule blocking it. </p></li><li><p>The third is <b>the impact when a vulnerability is inevitably exploited. </b>No architecture catches everything. After the vulnerability is exploited, the question we ask ourselves is: where can the attacker get to with one identity, one path, or one credential, before something else stops them? If the answer is &quot;anywhere they want,&quot; the vulnerability was never the problem. The architecture around the vulnerability was.</p></li></ul>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="cloudflares-superpower-visibility">Cloudflareâs superpower: visibility</h2>
      <a href="#cloudflares-superpower-visibility" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>We see roughly a fifth of the world&#39;s web traffic and that traffic tells us, in real time, which payloads are mutating, which patterns are picking up, and where attacker tooling is moving next. Two teams turn that visibility into defense.</p><p>First is <a href="https://www.cloudflare.com/cloudforce-one/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><b><u>Cloudforce One</u></b></a>, our threat intelligence, research, and operations team, which sits within the Cloudflare security organization. They turn what we see across the network into insights the rest of the stack can act on: tracked adversaries, emerging campaigns, and indicators of compromise (IOCs). The hard part of this work was never knowing what is malicious â it was the delay in mitigation. Knowledge of a new threat normally has to travel from a threat report, into a feed, and then into a companyâs defense before it can be used to block anything. Attackers have learned to move faster than that. Our network closes that gap: Cloudflare customers can now use Cloudforce One threat intelligence <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/realtime-threat-intel-waf-rules/"><u>directly within the WAF</u></a> to block high-risk traffic.</p><p>Second is the team that owns the WAF engine that does the actual detecting: the managed rulesets that run in front of our own properties and are available to every Cloudflare customer, the machine learning behind <a href="https://developers.cloudflare.com/waf/about/waf-attack-score/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><u>WAF Attack Score</u></a>, and the relationships that sometimes let us ship a rule before a CVE is publicly disclosed. The team is globally distributed and moves fast, releasing rules within hours of a proof-of-concept of an attack becoming known. Once a detection is deployed, it reaches our entire network, along with every Cloudflare customer, in under 30 seconds. <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/react2shell-rsc-vulnerabilities-exploitation-threat-brief/"><u>React2Shell</u></a> is a recent example: a managed WAF rule <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/waf-rules-react-vulnerability/"><u>was protecting our own properties, and everyone else&#39;s on Cloudflare</u></a>, hours before the official advisory was published.</p><p>The scoring layer, the defenses we put in front of the application, and the containment around the vulnerability all build on what these two teams see.Â </p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="scores-over-signatures">Scores over signatures</h2>
      <a href="#scores-over-signatures" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>Signature-based defenses were built for a world where novel exploits were scarce and variations took weeks. Cloudflare&#39;s traditional SLA from a fresh proof-of-concept to a live, deployed rule has been 12 hours. With the advent of frontier models, this is not good enough anymore. Detections need to be in place <a href="https://blog.himanshuanand.com/2026/05/the-90-day-disclosure-policy-is-dead/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><u>before a CVE is discovered</u></a>. This is why we layer ML-based detection in front of the traditional signature-based WAF.</p><p>The model is trained on a large body of past attack traffic, and it catches new variants of vulnerabilities before they&#39;re publicly known. A novel <a href="https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/security/threats/sql-injection/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><u>SQL injection</u></a> or remote code execution chain is almost always a rearrangement of attack shapes the model has seen before, even when the specific exploit is brand new. We run the model on every request and assign a WAF Attack Score between 1 and 99, based on how closely the request resembles those underlying shapes, not against a list of known-bad signatures. The lower the score, the more aggressively we treat the request. That score determines whether we let the request through. We apply a similar scoring methodology to AI prompts with <a href="https://developers.cloudflare.com/waf/detections/ai-security-for-apps/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><u>AI Security for Apps</u></a>: rather than check each prompt against a list of known malicious prompts, we score how closely a prompt resembles an actual attack.Â </p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="the-architecture-around-the-vulnerability">The architecture around the vulnerability</h2>
      <a href="#the-architecture-around-the-vulnerability" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>Those capabilities only matter once they&#39;re stacked in front of an application, and the first layer in our defense-in-depth approach is the <a href="https://www.cloudflare.com/en-gb/application-services/products/waf/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><b><u>WAF</u></b></a>. Anything that matches a known-bad pattern gets dropped before it reaches the application, which clears the bulk of the obvious traffic and lets the more specialized layers below focus on what&#39;s left.

On the API surface, we run a positive security model through <a href="https://www.cloudflare.com/en-gb/application-services/products/api-shield/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><b><u>API Shield</u></b></a>. Instead of trying to anticipate every bad request, we describe what a valid request to each API looks like, either from the API&#39;s own definition or learned from our real traffic, and anything that doesn&#39;t fit doesn&#39;t get through. This neutralizes the advantage of frontier AI models: because we only permit validated traffic, generating thousands of new attack variations fails to bypass the system.</p>
          <figure class="kg-card kg-image-card">
          <Image src="https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/5KSSyocxKzsJsOfG4pxOM7/059a597af1b67a3d5a5ccdeda5ab7ca1/image2.png" alt="" class="kg-image" width="1942" height="1482" loading="lazy"/>
          </figure><p><sup><i>Cloudflareâs layered architecture</i></sup></p><p><a href="https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/bots/what-is-bot-management/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><b><u>Bot Management</u></b></a> catches probing traffic on our network before frontier models can build a map. It scores every request on how likely it is to be automated, using the same signals across our whole network: how the client behaves, whether it looks like a real browser, and whether the connection matches a known-bad pattern. An attack only lands if it can find a soft spot.Â </p><p><a href="https://www.cloudflare.com/sase/products/access/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><b><u>Zero Trust Network Access</u></b></a> is used for every internal application. The implicit trust of being inside the network is replaced with explicit per-request identity and policy for every employee accessing every tool. The value of this was clear when one of our engineers shipped a misconfigured tool. A flat network would have exposed everything on the same segment, but in our deployment, the exposure stopped at the tool itself. We built <a href="https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/access-controls/access-settings/require-access-protection/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><b><u>Require Access Protection</u></b></a> afterwards so newly deployed or misconfigured applications can&#39;t be reachable before an access policy is in place.</p><p><a href="https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/integrations/identity-providers/idp-federation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><b><u>IdP Federation</u></b></a> makes that secure by default posture easier to keep consistent across every Cloudflare account â which becomes even more necessary when more people are shipping internal tools quickly. Instead of asking each team to wire up SSO separately, we configure our identity provider (IdP) once and share it across the organization. New accounts get SSO automatically, recipient-side IdP connections are read-only, and Access policies in each account still evaluate the resulting identity as part of the normal request flow.Â </p><p><a href="https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/access-controls/ai-controls/mcp-portals/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><b><u>MCP Server Portal</u></b></a> gives teams a controlled way to connect AI agents to enterprise systems. Agents access MCP servers that are centrally managed through a single portal, with every action logged. That way when an agent acts on someone&#39;s behalf, we know what it did, what it touched, and whether it should have been allowed to. The full picture of how we built it is in our post on <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/enterprise-mcp/"><u>enterprise MCP</u></a>.</p><p><a href="https://www.cloudflare.com/developer-platform/products/ai-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><b><u>AI Gateway</u></b></a> runs in front of our internal AI tools the same way <a href="https://developers.cloudflare.com/waf/detections/ai-security-for-apps/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><u>AI Security for Apps</u></a> runs in front of customer-facing AI features, with the same scoring and the same visibility. Inside the company, the visibility piece is more useful than the blocking, because we needed to see what engineers were actually building before we could write meaningful policy on it.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="where-your-teams-can-start">Where your teams can startÂ </h2>
      <a href="#where-your-teams-can-start" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>Frontier models can help attackers find vulnerabilities, adapt payloads, and move faster, but they still have to pass through the layered defense you deploy in front of your application. That is where teams should start:</p><ul><li><p>Put inspection in front of public applications.</p></li><li><p>Define what valid API traffic looks like.</p></li><li><p>Use bot detection to limit automated probing.</p></li><li><p>Require identity and access policy before any internal tool is reachable.</p></li></ul><p>For AI and agentic systems:</p><ul><li><p>Route model traffic through a gateway.</p></li><li><p>Keep agents connected through approved MCP servers.</p></li><li><p>Log what they do.Â </p></li></ul><p>The goal is to make sure that when one layer misses, the next layer limits what the attacker can see, reach, or change.</p><p>That is the point of the architecture around the vulnerability: to limit the scope of an attack. The vulnerability may be what starts the attack, but the architecture determines how far it can go.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="how-do-we-know-this-approach-works">How do we know this approach works?</h2>
      <a href="#how-do-we-know-this-approach-works" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>Plenty of security stacks look impenetrable on a whiteboard but fall over in practice. That is why we test ours continuously, both at the perimeter and inside our environment, with our red team involved across both.</p><p><b>At the perimeter</b>, frontier models are one tool we use to test our application security stack as an adaptive attacker. These models sit alongside the rest of our red team and detection workflows including: manual testing, threat intelligence, observed traffic patterns, proof-of-concept analysis, and signals from our own network. Together, those inputs help us decide where to aim testing: newly launched products, recently changed surfaces, and the paths an attacker is most likely to probe first. The most important part is the process that follows. When something gets through, we identify the gap, use the right mix of tools to understand it, write the rule or mitigation, ship the update, and test again to make sure the gap is closed.</p><p><b>Inside the environment,</b> our red team starts from the assumption that the perimeter has already failed. They look at what has changed, where sensitive systems carry risk, and whether one compromised identity, path, or credential can reach farther than it should. When we change the architecture based on what they find, they run the scenario again against the new version to confirm the gap is actually closed.</p><p>We confirm that this architecture is working by continuously testing its behavior during failures, rather than relying on the perfection of individual layers.</p><p>If your team is working on the same problems and would like to compare notes, reach out to us at <a href="#"><u><span class="__cf_email__" data-cfemail="6e1d0b0d1b1c071a17430f07431c0b1d0b0f1c0d062e0d02011b0a08020f1c0b400d0103">[email&#160;protected]</span></u></a>.</p></div></section><section class="post-full-content flex flex-row flex-wrap mw7 center mb3"><div class="post-content lh-copy w-100 gray1 bt b--gray8 pt3"><span></span></div></section><astro-slot> <!--[if astro]>server-island-start<![endif]--><script data-cfasync="false" src="/cdn-cgi/scripts/5c5dd728/cloudflare-static/email-decode.min.js"></script><script type="module" data-astro-rerun data-island-id="c36f58e5-b26a-4ae7-8fca-9602b57040d1">const headers = new Headers({});
